Eigen-Spike Emergence and Quadratic Equivalents for Conjugate Kernels on Nonlinearly Separable Data
Collin Cranston ∗ Department of Mathematics University of California, San Diego, USA ccransto@ucsd.edu Zhichao Wang ∗ ICSI and Department of Statistics University of California, Berkeley, USA zhichao.wang@berkeley.edu Todd Kemp Department of Mathematics University of California, San Diego, USA tkemp@ucsd.edu Michael W. Mahoney ICSI, LBNL and Department of Statistics University of California, Berkeley, USA mmahoney@stat.berkeley.edu

Abstract
Recent work in random matrix theory (RMT) has developed the notion of deterministic equivalents: typically linear surrogate models that approximate the spectral behavior of large nonlinear random matrices, such as nonlinear feature maps in neural networks (NNs). On the one hand, these deterministic equivalents make theoretical predictions tractable by reducing a complex model to a simpler model with properties that fall under the umbrella of classical RMT tools. However, this leaves open the question of whether this idealized linear equivalence remains meaningful when dealing with high-dimensional nonlinearly separable data, such as performing clssification on nonlinearly separable data. Motivated by this, we consider the conjugate kernel (CK), which is the nonlinear feature map of a feedforward NN, under a canonical nonlinearly separable dataset, the XOR problem; and we use the study of informative outlier eigenvalues in the CK and whether their corresponding eigenvectors asymptotically align with XOR labels as a proxy for nonlinear learnability. We develop a robust quadratic equivalent to the spiked CK matrix that enables a precise analysis of emergent informative spikes, as one modifies various knobs common in ML practice: sample complexity, signal-to-noise ratio (SNR), nonlinear activation choice, and pretrained features. In each of these scenarios, we derive a precise BBP-type phase transition in which linear classification via the CK eigenvectors becomes possible. Our analysis helps translate the power of deterministic equivalence tools in RMT to study problems of practical relevance in ML.

footnotetext:

Equal contribution.

1
Introduction

Spectral information—from weight matrices, kernel matrices, and Hessians—provides a quantitative window into representation learning and efficient optimization in neural networks (NNs): e.g., eigenvalues in weight matrices of NNs capture the strength of learned directions, while corresponding eigenvectors indicate which features in the data are amplified or extracted during training
[
59
,
57
,
58
,
60
,
87
,
52
,
80
,
3
,
73
,
32
]
.
A major direction in deep learning theory is to use spectral tools to understand when a network can
propagate, preserve, transform, and amplify
task-relevant structure that may be hidden in high-dimensional datasets.

A concrete and widely used spectral mechanism is the emergence of
spikes
(outlier eigen/singular values) and the
alignment
of their eigen/singular vectors with signal directions in the dataset.
Random matrix theory (RMT) provides a powerful tool to analyze such eigen-spike emergence.
In high-dimensional spiked models where a low-rank signal is embedded in Gaussian noise, BBP (Baik–Ben Arous–Péché) transitions characterize when an informative outlier separates from the bulk spectrum and when its associated eigenvector becomes correlated with the underlying signal
[
7
,
63
]
. Recent work has developed nonlinear counterparts for random-feature and kernel-type matrices, providing sharp predictions for outlier locations and eigenvector alignment
[
82
,
30
,
4
,
81
]
. Such nonlinear RMT can be applied to study deep learning theory, e.g., the infinite-width
Neural Tangent Kernel
(NTK) and the
Conjugate Kernel
(CK)
[
44
,
68
,
2
]
for multi-layer NNs.
Their high-dimensional spectra have been studied extensively
[
53
,
82
,
50
,
50
]
. In this work, we focus on the CK, and we study
emergent spikes
induced by
nonlinear
structure in the data.
Concretely, we consider the following spectral question for CK:

When can NNs transfer a nonlinear pattern in the data into a linearly accessible direction (an aligned eigen-spike) that enables linear classification?

In this paper, the CK is defined by a one-hidden-layer random NN:

\bm{Y}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\,\sigma(\bm{W}\bm{X})\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times n},
with nonlinear activation
\sigma
, random weights
\bm{W}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times d}
, and data
\bm{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times n}
. The
CK matrix is the Gram matrix of hidden activations,

\bm{K}=\bm{Y}^{\top}\bm{Y}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
for
n
data points. The spectral distribution of
\bm{K}
is particularly useful for generalization error of random feature regression, as demonstrated in
[
61
,
42
]
for isotropic Gaussian data
\bm{X}
. However, real datasets often contain low-rank or nonlinear structure that may be hard to analyze using PCA
[
46
,
74
,
77
,
72
,
8
,
18
,
45
]
. To highlight genuinely nonlinear structure, we instead take
\bm{X}
to be the high-dimensional
XOR Gaussian mixture
dataset (Section
3
). This dataset is a balanced four-component Gaussian mixture whose two classes are unions of opposite components
[
69
]
. The key property is that XOR is
not linearly separable
in first-order statistics: the class means vanish, and any method that only “sees” linear correlations cannot recover labels. However, XOR is
quadratically separable
, so a second-order feature can potentially make the task linearly solvable. Heuristically, the eigenvalues of
\bm{K}
describe the spectrum of learned similarities, while the alignment of leading eigenvectors with task structure (e.g., labels or cluster indicators) may lead to spectral methods solving the task.
Therefore, this paper aims to answer the following spectral question for
\bm{K}
:

When do outlier eigenvalues emerge in the CK spectrum, and when do their spiked eigenvectors align with the XOR labels and enable linear classification?

In practice, NN performance is affected by hyperparameters, dataset structure, and architecture; and, as a practical matter, machine learning (ML) practitioners typically fiddle (quite aggressively) with various “knobs” of the ML training process in order to improve model performance.
Motivated by this, we consider properties of the CK matrix for the XOR problem, as various “knobs” for
\bm{K}
are varied; and, depending on their values, we observe very different spectral behavior.
Concretely, we vary the following knobs (which are among the standard parameters that are tuned in ML practice): sampling size scaling
n
with input features
d
and NN width
N
; signal-to-noise ratio (SNR) in the data
\bm{X}
; the pretrained weight matrix
\bm{W}
; and the choice of nonlinear activation
\sigma
.
We show that, depending on the values of these “knobs,” one can obtain qualitatively different spectral properties, various phase transitions, and the emergence of
quadratic informative spikes
in the CK matrix.
Importantly, the emergence of these quadratically informative spikes makes the nonlinear XOR linearly classifiable.

Figure 1
:
Finite-SNR proportional regime: failure of linear classification for XOR.

We consider the CK matrix
\bm{K}
in the regime
n\asymp d\asymp N
with a finite SNR.

Left:
kernel-PCA visualization of the samples using the top two principle components (PCs) of
\bm{K}
(each point is one sample, colored by its true binary label).
The four visible clouds form the canonical XOR geometry: positive and negative classes occupy alternating clusters, so the leading two-dimensional spectral representation is still not linearly separable.

Right:
empirical eigenvalue histogram of
\bm{K}
(blue bars) together with the theoretical prediction by Theorem
3
(red curve).
The red
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathbf{\times}}
marks the predicted location of the two outlier eigenvalues of
\bm{K}
with the same value by Theorem
3
; the arrow links the subfigure of the first two outlier eigenvectors plotted against sample index, whose four-block pattern tracks mixture components rather than the binary labels.
Thus the outlier eigenspace is asymptotically orthogonal to the XOR label vector
{\bm{y}}
.
Simulation parameters:
n=5000
,
N=d=15000
, SNR
r=6.0
, and activation
\sigma(x)\propto\mathrm{ReLU}
.

1.1
Our Approach

Much of the existing RMT literature on the CK matrix analysis lies in a
proportional regime (
n\asymp d\asymp N
), and it often involves the assumption of isotropic Gaussian data
\bm{Z}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times n}
.
In this setting, a standard tool is a

linear equivalent
(LE) for the
feature matrix

\sigma(\bm{W}\bm{Z})
.
This approach replaces the nonlinear features
with an affine Gaussian surrogate and an independent Gaussian noise term
\bm{N}
:

\sigma(\bm{W}\bm{Z})\;\approx\;b_{\sigma}\,\bm{W}\bm{Z}\;+\;a_{\sigma}\,\bm{N},\qquad\bm{N}_{ij}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,1),

(1.1)

for constants
a_{\sigma},b_{\sigma}
determined by
\sigma
.
This approximation is also called
Gaussian equivalence

in prior work
[
61
,
42
,
35
,
15
]
.
When applying the LE, one can show that the spectra of nonlinear CK matrices are
asymptotically equivalent
to a
linear
random matrix model, thus making classical RMT directly applicable.
However, the LE also suggests that the induced kernel behaves essentially like a

linear
kernel plus isotropic noise; this can hide important nonlinear task structure; see Figure
1
.

For the XOR problem, the nonlinearity
\sigma
is essential: it can transform a nonlinear classification task into one that is linearly accessible.
To understand this, we vary the ML “knobs” listed above to identify regimes in which the LE approximation works, versus when it breaks down.
To go beyond the classical LE in (
1.1
), we develop a refined (quadratic) equivalent model that captures emergent spiked eigenvalues and eigenvectors carrying nonlinear information about CK and the dataset, thereby enabling linear classification on the nonlinear XOR problem.
We refer to such spikes as
quadratic informative spikes
.
Addressing these questions will clarify the regimes in which NNs, and in particular various choices for training knobs of the ML training process, can recover and even amplify beneficial nonlinear features concealed in the dataset.

Quadratic Equivalents (QE) .

We model the XOR data as a low-rank
perturbation
\bm{X}=\bm{Z}+\bm{M}
, where
\bm{Z}
is Gaussian noise and
\bm{M}
is a rank-two signal that encodes the XOR structure. Our goal is to understand how this signal
shows up in the CK spectrum. We go beyond the LE in (
1.1
), which captures only the bulk spectrum, and derive a QE that precisely tracks all spikes via
a second-order Taylor approximation of
\sigma
.
At a high level, this yields the decomposition

\displaystyle\sigma(\bm{W}\bm{X})

\displaystyle\;\approx\;\underbrace{\sigma(\bm{W}\bm{Z})}_{\text{bulk+uninformative spikes}}\;+\;\underbrace{\bm{T}_{1}}_{\text{linear spikes}}\;+\;\underbrace{\bm{T}_{2}}_{\text{quadratic spikes}}

(1.2)

where
\bm{T}_{1}
and
\bm{T}_{2}
are finite-rank contributions induced by the signal

\bm{M}
and
\sigma
(see (
5.1
) and Proposition
8
).
In this QE, there are three terms.
The first term,
\sigma(\bm{W}\bm{Z})
, determines the bulk spectrum; and it may also possibly induce
uninformative outliers
, solely due to the architecture
[
12
]
.
The second term,
\bm{T}_{1}
, corresponds to a linear transformation of the signal
\bm{M}
; while it can induce outliers, their eigenvectors turn out
not
to be aligned with the XOR labels, meaning that they are not useful for nonlinear XOR classification.
The third term,
\bm{T}_{2}
, captures quadratic properties: depending on the correct tuning of the ML training “knobs,” this quadratic term
\bm{T}_{2}
can dominate and create
quadratic informative spikes
(see (
5.1
)).
This effectively provides a “quadratic feature channel” that makes the nonlinear XOR problem analyzable with RMT and linearly separable.

1.2
Our Contributions

We develop a RMT framework for the CK that is based on a QE and that captures when nonlinear
learnability emerges for XOR, in regimes where the LE behavior is insufficient.
We are particularly interested in this emergence as a function of the following four “knobs” that are widely-used by ML practitioners.

1.

Finite-SNR proportional limit (
n\asymp d
): two negative results.

In the proportional regime with
\mathrm{SNR}=\Theta(1)
, CK can exhibit outlier
eigenvalues, but their eigenvectors do
not
align with XOR labels; hence spectral methods with linear readouts on CK features fail to classify XOR
(Theorem
3
; Figure
1
).
We also prove failure for Euclidean distance kernel spectral clustering.

2.

Large-SNR proportional limit: emergence of quadratic informative spike.

We show that with increasing SNR scaling and with proper activation choice, a pair of informative outliers separates and aligns with the XOR class direction, enabling linear spectral clustering/classification
(Theorem
5
).

3.

Pretrained/spiked features: test-time BBP transition.

A low-rank perturbation of a random
\bm{W}
can yield the emergence of an outlier whose eigenvector aligns with the induced task
direction (Theorem
6
) even for finite-SNR and proportional limit. Then, empirically, we show that linear spectral clustering occurs for CK matrix with weights from a pretrained NN on CIFAR-2.

4.

Quadratic sample-size regime (
n\asymp d^{2}
): a quadratic kernel.

We prove an operator-norm reduction of the CK to a quadratic polynomial kernel in this regime for ultra-wide NNs; with proper activation choice, the reduced model has an informative outlier direction to recover XOR labels (Theorem
7
).

Sample

\Theta(n^{1/4})

\mathbf{W}_{1}
)

\Theta(1)

\Theta(1)

Yes

Table 1
:
Summary of different knobs. From Case 2–4,
c_{\sigma}\neq 0
is
necessary
in all these cases. If
c_{\sigma}=0
(e.g., tanh after centering/normalization), linear classification via CK is impossible.

2
Additional Literature Review

Simplicity bias and low-rank structure.

Recent work suggests that deep networks and transformers exhibit an implicit

distributional simplicity bias
, learning simpler (lower-order)
statistical structure of the data earlier in training

[
9
,
70
]
. Complementarily,

[
43
]
report a
low-rank simplicity bias
in deep networks,
arguing that depth/over-parameterization can implicitly favor low effective-rank
feature representations. From a broader perspective,

[
85
]
argues that many generalization phenomena often viewed
as “mysterious” in deep learning can be reconciled with classical theory via
soft inductive biases.

Spectrum of kernel random matrices.

The study of kernel random matrices and their spectral distributions has become a major
topic in RMT.
Global convergence of the empirical spectral distribution (ESD) is known for
broad classes of nonlinear kernels

[
26
,
17
,
25
]
. In the proportional regime,
[
17
]
and
[
25
]

characterize limiting spectra of inner-product kernels.
The CK and NTK can be viewed as random kernels, whose spectra were
further studied
[
29
,
2
,
67
,
68
,
21
,
39
]
and connected to generalization in random feature regression

[
61
,
2
]
and memorization of spurious feature
[
14
]
.

Nonlinear spiked covariance matrices.

Spiked models exhibit phase transitions where informative eigenvalues separate
from the bulk
[
7
,
66
]
. Recent work extends this
to
nonlinear
random matrices, i.e., matrices whose entries are nonlinear
functions of high-dimensional random projections (such as
\sigma(\bm{W}\bm{X})
),
leading to nonlinear analogues of BBP transitions and alignment formulas

[
82
,
30
,
4
,
81
,
38
,
12
]
.
Outlier eigen-structure of CK and NTK have been studied
[
49
]
, using the deterministic equivalence from
[
37
]
.
Although they can deal with random deep networks and general Gaussian mixture datasets, their CKs and NTKs are expected kernels (namely first take width
N\to\infty
) and only equivalent to linear kernels.

Polynomial sample-size regimes.

In the proportional regime (
n\asymp d
), LE/Gaussian-equivalence phenomena
often imply linear-kernel behavior for broad families of kernels. Moving to
polynomial regimes (
n\asymp d^{k}
), several works show that many kernel matrices
behave like polynomial kernels of degree
k
(with rigorous deterministic
equivalents and sharp asymptotics)

[
86
,
55
,
16
,
62
]
.
In the quadratic regime (
k=2
), nonlinear kernels can reduce in operator norm
to quadratic polynomial kernels
[
33
,
65
]
, enabling classification of quadratically separable data such as XOR

[
69
]
.
[
84
]
recently proved a conditional Gaussian equivalence for random feature model at quadratic regime to show the asymptotic generalization error in this case.

Gaussian mixtures and XOR problem.

Gaussian mixture classification, specifically the XOR mixture has become an important testbed for statistics and ML to evaluate various algorithms.

[
69
]
documented a sharp contrast between trained
two-layer networks and kernel methods on such mixtures, motivating a spectral
analysis of when (and why) kernels can succeed.
Related RMT analyses for mixture
classification with kernels appear in
[
51
,
20
]
.

[
34
]
showed how training two layer NNs with SGD can learn this XOR problem even with a sample complexity
\tilde{O}(d)
.
Recently,
[
76
]
analyzed continual learning by studying one-hidden-layer quadratic NNs trained with gradient descent on an XOR dataset.
[
23
]
analyzed two-layer networks after one gradient step on
\bm{W}
with Gaussian-mixture data using higher-order polynomial equivalence.
This motivates our spiked feature case in Section
4.3
, while our result focuses on label-aligned outliers for linear classification on XOR, unlike trained feature regression tasks in
[
23
]
.

3
Notations and Preliminaries

Settings of One-layer NN model.

We study the output of one-layer NNs when fed a dataset
\bm{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times n}
.
Here,
d
is the input dimension and
n
is the sample size.
We consider different scalings between
n
and
d
to determine the sample complexity of the model.
Let the weight matrix at random initialization be
\bm{W}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times d}
with
[\bm{W}]_{ij}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,1)
for
i\in[N],j\in[d]
, where
N
is the width of NN.
Denote the output of our one-layer NN and the CK matrix as

\bm{Y}:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sigma(\bm{W}{\bm{X}}),\qquad\bm{K}:=\bm{Y}^{\top}\bm{Y},

(3.1)

where
\sigma
is a nonlinear activation. Our target is the eigenstructure of this CK when
\bm{X}
has a nonlinear separable pattern.
We now introduce the assumptions of the activation function
\sigma
.

Assumption 1
.

Assume
\sigma
is three-times differentiable with

\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}|\sigma^{\prime}(x)|,|\sigma^{\prime\prime}(x)|,|\sigma^{\prime\prime\prime}(x)|\leq\lambda_{\sigma}
, for some
\lambda_{\sigma}\in(0,\infty)
, and is centered and normalized with respect to

\xi\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\sigma(\xi)]=0,\qquad\mathbb{E}[\sigma(\xi)^{2}]=1.

(3.2)

Define the first and second order Hermite coefficients of
\sigma
as

\displaystyle b_{\sigma}

\displaystyle:=\mathbb{E}[\sigma^{\prime}(\xi)]=\mathbb{E}[\xi\,\sigma(\xi)]\in\mathbb{R},

(3.3)

\displaystyle c_{\sigma}

\displaystyle:=\mathbb{E}[\sigma^{\prime\prime}(\xi)]=\mathbb{E}[(\xi^{2}-1)\sigma(\xi)]\in\mathbb{R}.

(3.4)

Many smooth activations satisfy Assumption
1
, after centering and
normalization (e.g.,
\tanh
,
\mathrm{erf}
, smoothed-ReLU, GELU). The condition

b_{\sigma}
can be zero, which is
needed for the pure-quadratic-channel regime in
Theorem
5
. While ReLU is not
C^{3}
, it can be handled by smoothing
or by approximation arguments; we keep Assumption
1
to avoid
technical distractions.

XOR Dataset.

The XOR dataset is a binary class Gaussian Mixture dataset with four balanced clusters. We denote the collection of
n
data points by
\bm{X}=[{\bm{x}}_{1},\ldots,{\bm{x}}_{n}]\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times n}
, where

{\bm{x}}_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\frac{1}{4}\sum_{a=1}^{4}\mathcal{N}(\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}{\bm{\mu}}_{a},\frac{1}{d}\mathbf{I}_{d}).

Without loss of generality, we reorder the data points so that we can define

\bm{X}=\bm{M}+\bm{Z},

(3.5)

where
\bm{Z}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times n}
has i.i.d. entries with distribution
\mathcal{N}(0,\frac{1}{d})
and

\bm{M}=r\sqrt{\frac{n}{2d}}\big({\bm{u}}_{1}\cdot\bm{v}_{1}^{\top}+{\bm{u}}_{2}\cdot\bm{v}_{2}^{\top}\big),

(3.6)

for some
r\geq 0
, with unit-norm vectors

{\bm{u}}_{1}:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{1}_{d/2}\\
-\mathbf{1}_{d/2}\end{bmatrix},\quad{\bm{u}}_{2}:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{1}_{d/2}\\
\mathbf{1}_{d/2}\end{bmatrix},\quad{\bm{v}}_{1}=\sqrt{\frac{2}{n}}\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{1}_{n/4}\\
-\mathbf{1}_{n/4}\\
\mathbf{0}_{n/2}\end{bmatrix},\quad{\bm{v}}_{2}=\sqrt{\frac{2}{n}}\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{0}_{n/2}\\
\mathbf{1}_{n/4}\\
-\mathbf{1}_{n/4}\end{bmatrix}.

(3.7)

Here, the signal-to-noise ratio
(\mathrm{SNR})
is defined as
\mathrm{SNR}:=r^{2}
. In this case, the mean of each data point is defined by

{\bm{\mu}}_{1}=r\cdot{\bm{u}}_{1},\quad{\bm{\mu}}_{2}=r\cdot{\bm{u}}_{2},\quad{\bm{\mu}}_{3}=-{\bm{\mu}}_{1},\quad{\bm{\mu}}_{4}=-{\bm{\mu}}_{2},

Following the above definition of
\bm{X}
, we consider a binary classification dataset
\{({\bm{x}}_{i},y_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
with
y_{i}\in\{\pm 1\}
.
We assume the dataset is not linearly separable in the input space, i.e., there do not exist
{\bm{w}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
b\in\mathbb{R}
such that

y_{i}({\bm{w}}^{\top}{\bm{x}}_{i}+b)>0\quad\forall i\in[n].

For simplicity, in this case, the labels of data
\bm{X}
are defined by

\bm{y}^{\top}=\{y_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}=[\mathbf{1}_{n/2}^{\top},-\mathbf{1}_{n/2}^{\top}].

(3.8)

Our goal is to study the emergence of outlier eigenvalues in the spectrum of
\bm{K}=\bm{Y}^{\top}\bm{Y}
defined by (
3.1
) and (
3.5
), and verify whether the corresponding eigenvectors (spikes) are aligned to this label vector
{\bm{y}}
, in order to (partially) recover the classes of
\bm{X}
.

Marchenko–Pastur law.

Given sample covariance

\bm{S}=\bm{Z}^{\top}\bm{Z}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
defined in (
3.5
), assume
d,n\to\infty
with
\frac{n}{d}\to\psi\in(0,\infty)
. Then the ESD of
\bm{S}
converges weakly almost surely to the Marchenko–Pastur (MP) law
[
56
]
, denoted
\rho^{\mathrm{MP}}_{\psi}
.
Let
a\;=\;(1-\sqrt{\psi})^{2},
and
b\;=\;(1+\sqrt{\psi})^{2}.
When
\psi\leq 1
, the MP law has an absolutely continuous part on
[a,b]
with density

\rho^{\mathrm{MP}}_{\psi}(x)\;=\;\frac{1}{2\pi\psi x}\sqrt{(b-x)(x-a)}\;\mathbf{1}_{[a,b]}(x),

and, when
\psi>1
, an additional atom at zero
\rho^{\mathrm{MP}}_{\psi}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\frac{1}{\psi}}}\right)\delta_{0}+\rho^{\mathrm{MP}}_{\psi}(x)\,dx.

Stieltjes transform.

The Stieltjes transform provides an analytical tool for studying the ESD in RMT. For
z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus[0,\infty)
, define the Stieltjes transform of
\rho^{\mathrm{MP}}_{\psi}
by
m_{\psi}(z)\;=\;\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{1}{x-z}\,\rho^{\mathrm{MP}}_{\psi}(dx).

This Stieltjes transform fully captures the spectral distribution
\rho^{\mathrm{MP}}_{\psi}
, as we can recover the density from an inversion formula:

\rho^{\mathrm{MP}}_{\phi}(x)\;=\;\frac{1}{\pi}\lim_{\eta\downarrow 0}\operatorname{Im}m_{\phi}(x+i\eta).

Limiting spectral distribution of CK.

From
[
54
,
11
,
29
]
, we know the limiting ESD of
\bm{K}
is a deformed MP law, under certain conditions of
\bm{X}
and in the proportional regime. In general, we denote the deformed MP law as

\mu:=\rho^{\textnormal{MP}}_{\phi}\boxtimes\nu,\quad\nu:=(1-b_{\sigma}^{2})+b_{\sigma}^{2}\rho^{\textnormal{MP}}_{\psi},

(3.9)

where
\rho^{\textnormal{MP}}_{\phi}
is a standard MP law with parameter
\phi\in(0,\infty)
and
\nu
is a shift of MP law
\rho^{\textnormal{MP}}_{\psi}
with aspect ratio
\psi\in(0,\infty)
which is compactly supported in
[0,\infty)
. Here
\boxtimes
denotes free multiplicative convolution. We can fully characterize this deformed MP law
\mu
by its Stieltjes transform
m(z)=\int\frac{1}{x-z}d\mu(x)
. Notice that
\bm{Y}\bm{Y}^{T}
and
\bm{K}=\bm{Y}^{\top}\bm{Y}
share the same non-zero eigenvalues. Hence, we can also define a limit measure for the ESD of
\bm{Y}\bm{Y}^{T}
by
\tilde{\mu}
whose the Stieltjes transform is denoted by
\tilde{m}(z)
, which we will refer to as the companion Stieltjes transform of
m(z)
. Moreover, we have that
\widetilde{m}(z)=\frac{\phi-1}{z}+\phi m(z)
. We refer to
[
6
,
29
,
81
]
for more details of free multiplicative convolution and these Stieltjes transforms.
We denote the following transforms:

\displaystyle z(s)=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{s}+\phi\int\frac{x}{1+xs}\nu(dx),\qquad

\displaystyle\varphi(s)=

\displaystyle-\frac{sz^{\prime}(s)}{z(s)},

(3.10)

\displaystyle T(s)=

\displaystyle\frac{z(s)s^{2}-(\phi-1)s}{\phi},\qquad

\displaystyle T^{-1}(t)=

\displaystyle-\frac{t\bigl(1-\psi b_{\sigma}^{2}\,t\bigr)}{1+\bigl(1-\psi b_{\sigma}^{2}\bigr)t-\psi b_{\sigma}^{2}(1-b_{\sigma}^{2})t^{2}},

(3.11)

which will determine the outliers of
\bm{K}
. More properties of the transforms are given by Appendix
B.3
.

4
Main Results

In this section, we present our main results on the outlier eigenvalues and eigenvectors of the CK
\mathbf{K}
across several regimes.
In the first three regimes, we consider proportional limit, where
d,n,N\to\infty
with fixed aspect ratios, while varying other “knobs” discussed in Section
1
.
In particular:
Section
4.1
treats the finite
\mathrm{SNR}=\Theta(1)
regime;
Section
4.2
analyzes large
\mathrm{SNR}=\Theta(n^{1/4})
; and
Section
4.3
replaces the random weight matrix
\mathbf{W}
with a “pretrained” weight matrix modeled as a rank-one perturbation, still with
\mathrm{SNR}=\Theta(1)
.
Then, in Section
4.4
, we consider the quadratic sample-size regime
n\asymp d^{2}
, with finite SNR and random weights.
In each of these cases, we predict the emergence of outlier eigenvalues and whether their associated eigenvectors enable
linear
classification.

4.1
Proportional Limit with Finite SNR Case

Here, we consider a baseline regime in which all dimensions grow proportionally and the
\mathrm{SNR}
is finite.
We show that the outlier eigenvectors cannot be used to linearly classify the dataset, even partially. When
\mathrm{SNR}=\Theta(1)
, the structured XOR signal is too weak for
quadratic informative spikes to emerge in the CK spectrum under proportional scaling.

Assumption 2

(Proportional limit)
.

Let
n,d,N\to\infty
such that
n/N\to\phi\in(0,+\infty)
and
n/d\to\psi\in(0,+\infty)
for some fixed constants
\phi
and
\psi
.

We denote some constants used to define the BBP thresholds in the following theorem:

\displaystyle\tau:=\frac{c_{\sigma}^{2}}{2}\psi,\qquad\beta_{\rm lin}:=\frac{r^{2}\psi}{2}b_{\sigma}^{2},\qquad\tau_{\rm crit}:=b_{\sigma}^{2}\sqrt{\psi}(1+\sqrt{\psi}).

(4.1)

Recall the definition of
T(\cdot)
and
T^{-1}(\cdot)
in (
3.11
). Define

s_{\rm un}=T^{-1}(1/\tau),\quad s_{\rm lin}:=T^{-1}(1/\beta_{\rm lin})

when
\tau>0
and
\beta_{\rm lin}>0
, respectively.

Theorem 3
.

Under Assumptions
1
and
2
, we further assume that
r\geq 0
is a constant. Consider the
\bm{K}
with XOR data
\bm{X}
defined in Section
3
. Recall the probability measure
\mu
with transforms
z(\cdot),\varphi(\cdot)
and
T(\cdot)
defined by (
3.10
) and (
3.11
). Denote

{\bm{u}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\mathbf{1}_{n},\qquad{\bm{m}}:=\mathbb{E}[\sigma({\bm{w}}^{\top}\bm{Z})\mid\bm{Z}]\in\mathbb{R}^{n},\qquad{\bm{w}}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\bm{I}_{d}).

Assume in addition that
s_{\rm un}\neq s_{\rm lin}
if both are nozero.
Then the CK matrix
\bm{K}
satisfies the following.

(i)

(
Bulk.
) The ESD of
\bm{K}
converges weakly in probability to
\mu
defined in (
3.9
).

(ii)

(Uninformative outliers.)

If
c_{\sigma}\neq 0
and
z^{\prime}(s_{\rm un})>0
, then
\bm{K}
has two eigenvalues outside the support of
\mu
satisfying

\widehat{\lambda}_{{\rm un},1},\ \widehat{\lambda}_{{\rm un},2}=z(s_{\rm un})+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1).

Let
\widehat{}\bm{P}_{\rm un}
be the spectral projector onto this two-dimensional outlier eigenvalues. Then

\displaystyle\|\widehat{}\bm{P}_{\rm un}{\bm{u}}\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\varphi(s_{\rm un})+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1),

(4.2)

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\widehat{}\bm{P}_{\rm un}\frac{{\bm{m}}}{\|{\bm{m}}\|}}}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\varphi(s_{\rm un})}{\tau^{2}s_{\rm un}^{2}T^{\prime}(s_{\rm un})}+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1),

(4.3)

\displaystyle{\bm{u}}^{\top}\widehat{}\bm{P}_{\rm un}\frac{{\bm{m}}}{\|{\bm{m}}\|}

\displaystyle=o_{\mathbb{P}}(1).

(4.4)

Besides, when
b_{\sigma}=0
, (
4.2
) and (
4.3
) are asymptotically same.
If
c_{\sigma}=0
or
z^{\prime}(s_{\rm un})\leq 0
, then
\bm{K}
has no such uninformative outliers.

(iii)

(BBP of linear spikes from XOR.)

If
z^{\prime}(s_{\rm lin})>0
and
\beta_{\rm lin}>0
, then
\bm{K}
has additional two outlier eigenvalues outside the support of
\mu
satisfying

\widehat{\lambda}_{{\rm lin},1},\ \widehat{\lambda}_{{\rm lin},2}=z(s_{\rm lin})+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1),

and the spectral projector
\widehat{}\bm{V}_{\rm lin}
of the corresponding two-dimensional eigenspace satisfies

\big\|({\bm{v}}_{1}{\bm{v}}_{1}^{\top}+{\bm{v}}_{2}{\bm{v}}_{2}^{\top})\widehat{}\bm{V}_{\rm lin}\big\|_{F}^{2}=2\cdot\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-\frac{\varphi(s_{\rm lin})}{\beta_{\rm lin}^{\,2}s_{\rm lin}^{2}T^{\prime}(s_{\rm lin})}}}\right)+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1).

(4.5)

If
\beta_{\rm lin}=0
or
z^{\prime}(s_{\rm lin})\leq 0
, then
\bm{K}
has no such linear outliers.

(iv)

(No label alignment.)

Let
\widehat{}\bm{V}_{\rm out}
denote the orthogonal projector onto the direct sum of all separated outlier clusters described in
(ii)
–
(iii)
if they exist. Then, for the XOR labels
{\bm{y}}
,

\frac{1}{n}\|\widehat{}\bm{V}_{\rm out}{\bm{y}}\|^{2}=o_{\mathbb{P}}(1).

(4.6)

Theorem
3
exhibits a BBP-type phase transition for the CK matrix with XOR data: e.g.,
z^{\prime}(s_{\rm lin})>0
is the threshold showing when spikes separate from the deformed MP bulk
\mu
; in the separated case, the associated eigenvectors have nontrivial asymptotic overlap with certain directions.
There are up to four candidate spikes outside
\mathrm{supp}({\mu})
: two outliers (Theorem
3
(iii)) induced by the linear Hermite component of
\sigma
and spike strength
s_{\rm lin}
depending on
r
, and two potential “architectural” outliers (Theorem
3
(ii)) corresponding to
c_{\sigma}\neq 0

[
12
]
.
The latter is “uninformative” in the sense that it may persist even in the null model
r=0
(pure noise data).
See Section
6
for further discussion.
To focus on informative spikes, previous works always impose
c_{\sigma}=0

[
81
]
.
The proof of Theorem
3
is given in Appendix
D
.

Remark
.

For uninformative spikes in Theorem
3
(ii), there is a necessary condition for the BBP threshold
z^{\prime}(s_{\rm un})>0
for ununformative spikes to emerge:

z^{\prime}(s_{\rm un})>0\implies\tau>\tau_{\rm crit}.

Hence, uninformative spikes can be removed if
\tau\leq\tau_{\rm crit}
holds, namely
\sqrt{\psi}\bigl(\frac{c_{\sigma}^{2}}{2}-b_{\sigma}^{2}\bigr)\leq b_{\sigma}^{2}.
When the second Hermite coefficient
c_{\sigma}/\sqrt{2}
is smaller than the first
b_{\sigma}
in absolute value (for instance, a centered and normalized GELU function in Appendix
B.4
), this condition is satisfied for all
\psi>0
, and thus no uninformative spike emerges.

Remark
.

From the final statement of Theorem
3
in
(
4.6
), the separated outlier eigenvectors are
asymptotically orthogonal to the label vector
{\bm{y}}
.
Thus, these
outliers cannot be used for linear classification. Although the
spikes induced by
\lambda_{\rm lin}
carry information about the within-class
structure (alignment with
{\bm{v}}_{1},{\bm{v}}_{2}
in
(
4.5
)), they cannot recover the XOR labels
{\bm{y}}
without an additional nonlinear transform on spikes.

Kernel spectral clustering also fails.

We also consider classical kernel spectral clustering with an Euclidean distance kernel.
We focus in particular on the kernel spectral clustering algorithm introduced by
[
64
]
, using the leading (nontrivial) eigenvectors of the normalized Laplacian for clustering.

[
19
]
studied this kernel clustering for general Gaussian mixture data. The conclusion is
similar as above.
Full details are provided in Appendix
E
.

Theorem 4

(Kernel spectral clustering for XOR)
.

Let Assumption
2
hold, and let
r=\Theta(1)
. Consider the
kernel matrix
\big(\bm{K}^{(f)}\big)_{ij}=f\!\Bigl(\frac{1}{d}\|{\bm{x}}_{i}-{\bm{x}}_{j}\|^{2}\Bigr),
for
i,j\in[n],
and some
f(x)\in C^{3}
near
x=2
, with
f(2)>0
and
f^{\prime}(2)\neq 0
, and the
normalized Laplacian

\bm{L}=n\,\bm{D}^{-1/2}\bm{K}^{(f)}\bm{D}^{-1/2}
where
\bm{D}=\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diag}(\bm{K}^{(f)}\mathbf{1}_{n}).

Then,

•

(Isolated eigenvalues.)

If
r^{2}>2\sqrt{\psi^{-1}}
, then
\bm{L}
has two nontrivial isolated
eigenvalues
whose limits are given
by Theorem
48
in Appendix
E
.

•

(No label alignment.)

Let
\bm{\Pi}
be the orthogonal projector onto the span of the nontrivial isolated
eigenspace. Then,
\frac{1}{n}\,{\bm{y}}^{\top}\bm{\Pi}{\bm{y}}\;\to\;0
in probability.

4.2
Large-SNR Proportional Limit Case

Theorem
3
shows that spectral methods based on outlier eigenvectors fail to linearly classify XOR in the finite-SNR proportional regime. Linear informative spikes may still emerge, but a purely linear approximation of
\sigma
cannot capture the quadratic feature channel needed for XOR. To make XOR linearly learnable, we can increase the SNR so that the quadratic Hermite component of
\sigma
becomes non-vanishing.
Here, we confirm this: in the large-SNR regime, the additional two outliers created by the quadratic component become label-informative, enabling linear classification.

Recall
\tau
from (
4.1
). Let

r=r_{0}n^{1/4}
with constant
r_{0}\in(0,\infty)
. We further define the following constants:

\kappa_{0}:=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{\theta_{\mathrm{SNR}}^{4}}{n}=\frac{r_{0}^{4}\psi^{2}}{4},\qquad\beta_{\rm quad}:=c_{\sigma}^{2}\kappa_{0}.

(4.7)

For
\beta_{\rm quad}>0
, define the label factor

\qquad s_{y}:=T^{-1}(1/\beta_{\rm quad}).

(4.8)

When
c_{\sigma}\neq 0
, equivalently
\tau>0
and
\beta_{\rm quad}>0
, the two
possible
T
-values for the non-label factor are

t_{\pm}:=\frac{1\pm\sqrt{\beta_{\rm quad}/(\tau+\beta_{\rm quad})}}{\tau},\qquad s_{\pm}:=T^{-1}(t_{\pm}).

(4.9)

Theorem 5
.

Under Assumptions
1
and
2
, let
\bm{P}_{\rm lin}={\bm{v}}_{1}{\bm{v}}_{1}^{\top}+{\bm{v}}_{2}{\bm{v}}_{2}^{\top}
,
\bm{P}_{\perp}=\bm{I}-\bm{P}_{\rm lin}
, and
\bm{K}_{\perp}:=\bm{P}_{\perp}\bm{K}\bm{P}_{\perp}
. Then the following statements hold.

(i)

(Bulk.)
The ESD of
\bm{K}
converges
weakly in probability to
\mu
.

(ii)

(Diverging linear outliers.)
If
b_{\sigma}\neq 0
, then the top two eigenvalues of
\bm{K}
satisfy

\widehat{\lambda}_{1}(\bm{K}),\widehat{\lambda}_{2}(\bm{K})=\frac{b_{\sigma}^{2}r_{0}^{2}\psi}{2}\,n^{1/2}(1+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)),

and the associated 2D eigenspace projector
\widehat{}\bm{P}_{\rm lin}
satisfies
\|\widehat{}\bm{P}_{\rm lin}-\bm{P}_{\rm lin}\|_{\rm F}\xrightarrow{\mathbb{P}}0,
and
\frac{1}{n}{\bm{y}}^{\top}\widehat{}\bm{P}_{\rm lin}{\bm{y}}\xrightarrow{\mathbb{P}}0.

(iii)

(Order-one outliers of
\bm{K}_{\perp}
.)
Assume
c_{\sigma}\neq 0
, so

\tau,\beta_{\rm quad}>0
. Let
\mathcal{S}_{\rm cand}:=\{s_{+},s_{-},s_{y}\}

with
s_{\pm},s_{y}
defined by (
4.8
) and
(
4.9
). An element
s\in\mathcal{S}_{\rm cand}
produces a
separated order-one outlier of
\bm{K}_{\perp}
precisely when

z^{\prime}(s)>0,\qquad z(s)\in\mathbb{R}\setminus(\mathrm{supp}({\mu})\cup\{0\}).

(4.10)

More precisely, fix a limiting location
\lambda_{\star}\in\mathbb{R}\setminus(\mathrm{supp}({\mu})\cup\{0\})
, and define the algebraic multiplicity

m_{\star}:=\#\{s\in\mathcal{S}_{\rm cand}:z(s)=\lambda_{\star},\ z^{\prime}(s)>0\}.

(4.11)

Then for every sufficiently small deterministic interval

I_{\star}=(\lambda_{\star}-\delta,\lambda_{\star}+\delta)
containing no other
candidate location and disjoint from
\mathrm{supp}({\mu})
, the matrix
\bm{K}_{\perp}
has
exactly
m_{\star}
eigenvalues in
I_{\star}
, and all of them converge to

\lambda_{\star}
in probability.

Consequently,
\bm{K}_{\perp}
has at most three separated order-one outliers,
counting multiplicity.

(iv)

(Label alignment.)
Suppose that
s_{y}
is
admissible in the sense of (
4.10
). Let

\lambda_{y}:=z(s_{y})
, and let
\widehat{}\bm{P}_{y}^{\perp}
be the spectral projector of

\bm{K}_{\perp}
onto a small interval around
\lambda_{y}
. Then

\frac{1}{n}{\bm{y}}^{\top}\widehat{}\bm{P}_{y}^{\perp}{\bm{y}}\xrightarrow{\mathbb{P}}\gamma_{y}(s_{y}):=\frac{m_{\mu}(z(s_{y}))z^{\prime}(s_{y})}{\beta T^{\prime}(s_{y})}=-\frac{\varphi(s_{y})}{\beta^{2}s_{y}^{2}T^{\prime}(s_{y})}>0.

(4.12)

(v)

(Non-label clusters.)
If
\lambda_{\star}=z(s_{\pm})
is admissible from (
4.10
) and distinct from the label candidate
\lambda_{y}
, then the corresponding spectral projector

\widehat{}\bm{P}_{\rm nl,\star}^{\perp}
satisfies
\frac{1}{n}{\bm{y}}^{\top}\widehat{}\bm{P}_{\rm nl,\star}^{\perp}{\bm{y}}\xrightarrow{\mathbb{P}}0.

In contrast to Theorem
3
, when
r=\Theta(n^{1/4})
the linear outliers characterized by Theorem
3
become diverging.
Although Theorem
5
yields two diverging linear outliers, their eigenvectors remain asymptotically orthogonal to the label vector
{\bm{y}}
.
Meanwhile, the quadratic component of
\sigma
may produce three
O(1)
outliers (Theorem
5
(iii)).
Here, (
4.12
) gains a positive alignment with labels
{\bm{y}}
because the quadratic features
{\bm{v}}_{1}^{\odot 2}
and
{\bm{v}}_{2}^{\odot 2}
are preserved.
Hence, a linear readout on the
\lambda_{y}
-spike eigenspace in (
4.12
) can classify XOR. The proof of Theorem
5
is in Appendix
F
.

When
b_{\sigma}=0
, the diverging linear outliers vanish, simplifying spectral classification. If
c_{\sigma}=0
, then
\tau=\beta_{\rm quad}=0
, only the bulk and the possible two
diverging linear outliers in item
(ii)
remain. The absent quadratic component leads linear classification impossible, thus highlighting the role of the activation knob in preserving nonlinear structure.
For further discussion about the role of non-linearity choice, see Section
6
.

4.3
Pretrained Weight Matrix Case

Empirically, pretrained weight matrices in well-trained NNs often exhibit spectral spikes (and even heavy-tailed)
[
59
,
57
,
58
,
60
,
87
]
.
Such spectral behavior suggests that training induces nontrivial feature structure in the weights from dataset
[
3
,
4
,
23
]
.
However, the above theorems consider only random features, and therefore they do not capture task-dependent structure learned in the weight
\bm{W}
.
As a toy model for feature learning, we consider a simple but nontrivial pretrained model in which the weight is a rank-one perturbation of the random initialization:

\bm{W}_{1}=\bm{W}+\theta\,{\bm{a}}{\bm{b}}^{\top},

(4.13)

where
{\bm{a}}
and
{\bm{b}}
are independent of the
test
dataset
\bm{X}
.
A related rank-one structure arises, for example, after one-step gradient descent in certain student–teacher settings
[
3
,
23
]
, and it is also connected to LoRA
[
41
]
.
Below, we analyze a
test-time
BBP transition, i.e., we evaluate the CK built from
\bm{W}_{1}
on independent XOR test data
\bm{X}
, and we characterize the emergence and alignment of the induced spike.

Let
\mu,z,T,m_{\mu}
be the limiting law and transforms from
Theorem
3
. Recall
\tau
and
\beta_{\rm lin}
defined in (
4.1
). Set

\kappa:=1+\frac{r^{2}}{2},\qquad\eta:=3+3r^{2}+\frac{r^{4}}{2},\qquad\kappa_{\perp}:=\eta-\kappa^{2}=2+2r^{2}+\frac{r^{4}}{4},

(4.14)

\beta_{q}:=\frac{3c_{\sigma}^{2}}{4}\theta_{0}^{4}\phi\eta.\qquad\omega_{q}:=\frac{\kappa_{\perp}}{\eta},\qquad\chi_{y}:=\frac{r^{4}}{4\eta}.

(4.15)

Finally define, for
t\in\mathbb{C}
,

F_{q}(t):=(1-\tau t)^{2}-\beta_{q}t\,(1-\tau\omega_{q}t)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\frac{2}{3}\tau t}}\right).

(4.16)

Theorem 6
.

Suppose that
r\geq 0
is a constant. Let the weight matrix
\bm{W}_{1}
be defined as in (
4.13
) with

{\bm{b}}=\mathbf{1}_{d},\qquad{\bm{a}}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\bm{I}_{N}/N),\qquad\theta=\theta_{0}N^{1/4},

where
{\bm{a}}
is independent of
(\bm{W},\bm{X})
, and
\theta_{0}>0
is a fixed constant. Under Assumptions
1
and
2
, consider

\bm{Y}:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sigma(\bm{W}_{1}\bm{X}),\qquad\bm{K}:=\bm{Y}^{\top}\bm{Y},\qquad\bm{K}_{s}:=\bm{\Pi}_{s}\bm{K}\bm{\Pi}_{s},

where
\bm{\Pi}_{s}:=\bm{I}_{n}-\widehat{\bm{s}}\widehat{\bm{s}}^{\top},

\widehat{\bm{s}}:=\frac{{\bm{s}}}{\|{\bm{s}}\|},

{\bm{q}}:={\bm{s}}^{\odot 2},
and
{\bm{s}}:=\bm{X}^{\top}\mathbf{{\bm{b}}}
.
Then the following statements hold.

(i)

(Bulk.)

The empirical spectral distribution of
\bm{K}
converges weakly in probability
to
\mu
.

(ii)

(Diverging spike.)

If
b_{\sigma}\neq 0
, then

\widehat{\lambda}_{1}(\bm{K})=b_{\sigma}^{2}\theta_{0}^{2}\phi\kappa\sqrt{N}\,(1+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)).

(4.17)

If
\widehat{\bm{v}}_{1}
is the associated unit eigenvector, then

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle\widehat{\bm{v}}_{1},\widehat{\bm{s}}}}\right\rangle}}\right|^{2}\xrightarrow{\mathbb{P}}1,\qquad\frac{1}{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\widehat{\bm{v}}_{1}^{\top}{\bm{y}}}}\right|^{2}\xrightarrow{\mathbb{P}}0.

(4.18)

If
b_{\sigma}=0
, this diverging spike is absent.

(iii)

(Order-one outliers of
\bm{K}_{s}
.)

All order-one outliers of
\bm{K}_{s}
are given by the roots of the
three equations

1-\beta_{\rm lin}T(s)=0,\qquad 1-\frac{\beta_{\rm lin}}{\kappa}T(s)=0,\qquad F_{q}(T(s))=0.

(4.19)

Fix
\lambda_{\star}\in\mathbb{R}\setminus(\mathrm{supp}({\mu})\cup\{0\})
,
and let
m_{\star}
be the total algebraic multiplicity of all real solutions

s
of (
4.19
) such that

z(s)=\lambda_{\star},\qquad z^{\prime}(s)>0.

(4.20)

For every sufficiently small deterministic interval

I_{\star}=(\lambda_{\star}-\delta,\lambda_{\star}+\delta)
, disjoint from

\mathrm{supp}({\mu})\cup\{0\}
and containing no other candidate location,
\bm{K}_{s}
has
exactly
m_{\star}
eigenvalues in
I_{\star}
, and all of them converge to

\lambda_{\star}
in probability. If a real root satisfies
z^{\prime}(s)\leq 0
, it
does not produce a separated eigenvalue.

(iv)

(Label alignment.)

Roots coming only from the first two equations in
(
4.19
) have zero normalized label overlap. Suppose
that
\widehat{}\bm{P}_{\star}^{(s)}
is the spectral projector of
\bm{K}_{s}
onto

I_{\star}
where the roots of
F_{q}(T(s))=0
inside
I_{\star}
are simple. Then

\frac{1}{n}{\bm{y}}^{\top}\widehat{}\bm{P}_{\star}^{(s)}{\bm{y}}\xrightarrow{\mathbb{P}}\sum_{s\in\mathcal{R}_{q}(\lambda_{\star})}-\frac{m_{\mu}(z(s))z^{\prime}(s)}{T^{\prime}(s)}\frac{\beta_{q}\chi_{y}\,T(s)\,(1-\tau T(s))\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\frac{2}{3}\tau T(s)}}\right)}{F_{q}^{\prime}(T(s))}\geq 0,

(4.21)

where
\mathcal{R}_{q}(\lambda_{\star})
is the set of admissible roots of

F_{q}(T(s))=0
with
z(s)=\lambda_{\star},z^{\prime}(s)>0
.

Consequently
\bm{K}_{s}
has at most five separated order-one outliers, counting
multiplicity: two linear ones related to
\beta_{\rm lin}
and at most three roots from the coupled mean/covariance/quadratic block
F_{q}
.
This result shows that the large data-dependent spike in the pretrained weight
\bm{W}_{1}
amplifies the signals in XOR and alters the approximation: the usual LE is no longer sufficient, and a QE appears to capture the induced quadratic feature.
Theorem
6
(iv) shows this quadratic eigenvector aligns with the labels.
However, if
c_{\sigma}=0
, so
\beta_{q}=0
, then we do not obtain label alignment.
Empirical simulations of test-time BBP are presented in Section
6
. The proof of Theorem
6
is in Appendix
G
.

4.4
Quadratic Sample-size Case

In many ML settings, sample complexity dictates how many data points are sufficient
for an algorithm to succeed in high dimensions. In Section
4.1
, sample complexity
n=\Theta(d)
implies that LE removes higher-order structure, so the kernel behaves like a “linear” model. Now we change into the quadratic sample-size regime
n=\Theta(d^{2})
. Under this regime, we show that the CK behaves like a quadratic polynomial kernel
[
86
,
84
]

and thus becomes label-informative for XOR even at finite SNR.

For
i\in[n]
, set
s_{i}:=\|\bm{x}_{i}\|_{2}
,
\zeta_{0}^{(i)}:=\mathbb{E}[\sigma(s_{i}\xi)]
and
\zeta_{1}^{(i)}:=\mathbb{E}[\sigma(s_{i}\xi)\xi].

Define
\bm{\mu}_{\sigma}:=(\zeta_{0}^{(1)},\ldots,\zeta_{0}^{(n)})^{\top},
and
\bm{A}_{\sigma}:=\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diag}\!\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\zeta_{1}^{(1)}}{s_{1}},\ldots,\frac{\zeta_{1}^{(n)}}{s_{n}}}}\right).

Denote a subspace
\mathcal{U}_{X}:=\mathrm{span}\{\bm{1},\bm{\mu}_{\sigma}\}+\mathrm{Range}(\bm{A}_{\sigma}\bm{X}^{\top})\subset\mathbb{R}^{n},

and the orthogonal projector onto its complement
\bm{P}_{\sigma}:=\operatorname{Proj}(\mathcal{U}_{X}^{\perp}).

Theorem 7

(Quadratic sample-size CK at linear width)
.

Under Assumption
1
with
c_{\sigma}\neq 0
, consider the XOR model (
3.5
)–(
3.8
) with finite
r\in(0,\infty)
. Denote
\ell:=\frac{r^{4}}{4}
and
\lambda_{\mathrm{out}}(\gamma,\ell):=1+\gamma+\gamma\ell+\frac{1}{\ell}
. Assume that
\frac{n}{p}\to\gamma\in(0,\infty)
and
\frac{n}{N}\to\phi\in(0,\infty),
where

p:=\frac{d(d+1)}{2}
. Then the following hold for
\bm{K}_{\sigma}:=\bm{P}_{\sigma}\bm{K}\bm{P}_{\sigma}
.

(i)

(Bulk.)
The ESD of
\bm{K}_{\sigma}
converges
weakly to
\mu_{\rm q}=\rho_{\phi}^{\mathrm{MP}}\boxtimes\nu_{\rm q}
where
\nu_{\rm q}:=(1-b_{\sigma}^{2}-\frac{c_{\sigma}^{2}}{2})+\frac{c_{\sigma}^{2}}{2}\rho_{\gamma}^{\mathrm{MP}}
.

(ii)

(Spike of population CK.)
If

\ell>\gamma^{-1/2}
, then the population covariance
\mathbb{E}[\bm{K}|\bm{X}]
has a label-aligned outlier
\widehat{\Lambda_{y}}=\Lambda_{y}+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)
where
\Lambda_{y}:=(1-b_{\sigma}^{2}-\frac{c_{\sigma}^{2}}{2})+\frac{c_{\sigma}^{2}}{2}\lambda_{\mathrm{out}}(\gamma,\ell)
. Otherwise, no such outlier exists.

(iii)

(Spike for finite-width CK.)
If

\ell>\gamma^{-1/2}
and there is only one spike of the population CK with limiting value
\Lambda_{y}
, then the label produces a separated CK outlier
if and only if

z^{\prime}\!\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-\frac{1}{\Lambda_{y}}}}\right)>0.

(4.22)

In that case, for every sufficiently small deterministic interval
I_{y}
around

\lambda_{y}:=z\!\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-\frac{1}{\Lambda_{y}}}}\right)=\Lambda_{y}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\phi\int\frac{t}{\Lambda_{y}-t}\,\nu_{\rm q}(dt)}}\right),

(4.23)

which is disjoint from
\mathrm{supp}({\mu_{\rm q}})
and from all other
outlier locations,
\bm{K}_{\sigma}
has exactly one eigenvalue in
I_{y}
with probability
tending to one, and that eigenvalue converges in probability to
\lambda_{y}
.
If
\widehat{\bm{v}}_{y}
is the associated unit eigenvector, then

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle\widehat{\bm{v}}_{y},\frac{\bm{y}}{\sqrt{n}}}}\right\rangle}}\right|^{2}\xrightarrow{\mathbb{P}}\varphi\!\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-\frac{1}{\Lambda_{y}}}}\right)\frac{\gamma\ell^{2}-1}{\gamma\ell(\ell+1)}

(4.24)

where

\varphi\!\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-\frac{1}{\Lambda_{y}}}}\right)=\frac{1-\phi\int\frac{t^{2}}{(\Lambda_{y}-t)^{2}}\,\nu_{\rm q}(dt)}{1+\phi\int\frac{t}{\Lambda_{y}-t}\,\nu_{\rm q}(dt)}.

(4.25)

If either

\ell\leq\gamma^{-1/2}
or

z^{\prime}(-1/\Lambda_{y})\leq 0
, then no separated label-aligned eigenvalue of
\bm{K}_{\sigma}
is produced.

Theorem
7
only characterizes the label-aligned outlier generated by the XOR. The full CK may contain additional outliers: a
mean outlier,
d
linear-term outliers when
b_{\sigma}\neq 0
, and
possibly
o(n)
quadratic nuisance outliers caused by the rank-
O(d)

non-isotropic covariance component. These
outliers do not affect the limiting ESD and have asymptotically zero overlap with the XOR label. The proof is in Appendix
H
.

5
Proof Strategy for Main Results

Recall
\bm{X}=\bm{Z}+\bm{M}
, where
\bm{M}
is a rank-2 XOR signal and
\bm{Z}
is isotropic noise. The main idea is to decompose the CK into a bulk component (driven by
\bm{Z}
) and low-rank signal components (driven by
\bm{M}
). We first identify any uninformative spikes, then analyze the signal-induced spikes by improving standard tools from random matrix theory
[
10
]
.

In the proportional limit, for Theorems
3
,
5
, and
6
, we expand
\sigma(\bm{W}(\bm{Z}+\bm{M}))
around
\bm{W}\bm{Z}
and retain Hermite components of
\sigma
up to second order. This yields a bulk term
\sigma(\bm{W}\bm{Z})
and a low-rank spike decomposition for
\bm{Y}
.
Let
\bm{Y}_{0}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sigma(\bm{W}\bm{Z})
and
\theta_{\mathrm{snr}}=r\sqrt{\frac{n}{2d}}
.
Consider Gaussian random vectors
{\bm{g}}_{1}=\bm{W}{\bm{u}}_{1},\ {\bm{g}}_{2}=\bm{W}{\bm{u}}_{2}
.
Theorems
3
,
5
, and

6
repeatedly use the following QE:

\bm{Y}_{\mathrm{QE}}:=\bm{Y}_{0}+\underbrace{\frac{\theta_{\mathrm{snr}}b_{\sigma}}{\sqrt{N}}\Big({\bm{g}}_{1}{\bm{v}}_{1}^{\top}+{\bm{g}}_{2}{\bm{v}}_{2}^{\top}\Big)}_{\bm{T}_{1}\ \text{(linear spikes)}}+\underbrace{\frac{\theta_{\mathrm{snr}}^{2}c_{\sigma}}{\sqrt{N}}\Big({\bm{g}}_{1}^{\odot 2}{\bm{v}}_{1}^{\odot 2\top}+{\bm{g}}_{2}^{\odot 2}{\bm{v}}_{2}^{\odot 2\top}\Big)}_{\bm{T}_{2}\ \text{(quadratic spikes)}}.

(5.1)

Proposition 8

(QE for CK in Proportional Limit)
.

Assume
r=O(n^{1/4})
and Assumptions
1
and

2
hold. Let
\bm{Y}_{\mathrm{QE}}
be (
5.1
). Then, in probability,

\|\bm{Y}-\bm{Y}_{\mathrm{QE}}\|\ \to 0.

Consequently, if
\|\bm{Y}\|,\|\bm{Y}_{\mathrm{QE}}\|\lesssim 1
, then
\bm{K}=\bm{Y}^{\top}\bm{Y}
and
\bm{K}_{\mathrm{QE}}:=\bm{Y}_{\mathrm{QE}}^{\top}\bm{Y}_{\mathrm{QE}}

have asymptotically matching outlier eigenvalues and eigenvector alignments.

After this proposition, we can analyze informative spikes from
\bm{T}_{1}
and
\bm{T}_{2}
, although we have to carefully consider the uninformative spikes from the null model
\bm{Y}_{0}
, which gives more technical difficulty.
The magnitude of
\bm{T}_{1}
scales like
\theta_{\mathrm{snr}}b_{\sigma}
, while
\bm{T}_{2}
scales like

\theta_{\mathrm{snr}}^{2}c_{\sigma}/\sqrt{n}
. For finite SNR (or when
c_{\sigma}=0
), the quadratic spikes
\bm{T}_{2}
are negligible relative to the bulk. In this case, only the linear spikes in
\bm{T}_{1}
contribute; but these are uninformative for linear clustering.
In the large-SNR regime (with
c_{\sigma}\neq 0
),
\bm{T}_{2}
becomes comparable to the bulk. Then since
{\bm{y}}\in\mathrm{span}\{{\bm{v}}_{1}^{\odot 2},{\bm{v}}_{2}^{\odot 2}\}
, the corresponding outlier eigenvectors become label-informative.
Proposition
8
fails when we consider the quadratic sample-size regime
n=\Theta(d^{2})
in Section
4.4
. In Appendix
H.1
, we develop a different QE for the population CK matrix in this regime, which captures the concentration of the random kernel around a deterministic quadratic polynomial kernel.

6
Numerical Simulations for Varying Knobs

Figure 2
:
Large-SNR proportional regime: informative CK spikes recover the XOR labels.

We consider the same proportional limit as in Figure
1
, but now at large SNR
r=35
, where the spectral predictions of Theorem
5
imply the emergence of quadratic informative outliers. All other parameters are the same as in Figure
1
.

Left:
kernel-PCA visualization of the samples using the
3rd
and
4th
PCs of CK matrix
\bm{K}
(each point is one sample, colored by its true binary label).
In contrast to the finite-SNR case, these two spectral coordinates clearly separate the positive and negative classes by a linear boundary, showing that the informative outlier eigenspace now aligns with the XOR labels.

Right:
empirical eigenvalue histogram of
\bm{K}
(blue bars) together with the predicted curve from Theorem
5
(red curve).
The red
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathbf{\times}}
markers indicate the theoretical locations of the order-one informative outliers, and the arrows link the subfigure of the 3rd and 4th empirical eigenvectors (blue and green) plotted against sample index.
The block structure of the 4th eigenvector of
\bm{K}
matches the binary label split, explaining why the associated outlier eigenspace is informative for linear classification of XOR.
To focus on the order-one eigenvalues, we do not display the first two extremely large eigenvalues here for
\bm{K}
:

\widehat{\lambda}_{1}=155.6265
and
\widehat{\lambda}_{2}=152.6605
.

Our theorems highlight several control parameters (“knobs”) that govern whether outlier eigenvalues appear and whether the associated eigenvectors align with the XOR labels
{\bm{y}}
:

(1)
SNR
r
,
(2)
nonlinearity
\sigma
through parameters
(b_{\sigma},c_{\sigma})
in (
3.3
) and (
3.4
),
(3)
weight structure
\bm{W}
and
\bm{W}_{1}
(random/pretrained), and
(4)
scaling regime relating
(n,d,N)
.

6.1
Baseline Case: Proportional Limit with Finite SNR

Figure
1
depicts the random CK spectra with XOR in the finite-SNR regime and proportional limit (Assumption
2
), with theoretical spike predictions from Theorem
3
. Notice that in this simulation, because of the choice of
\sigma
, there are no uninformative spikes from Theorem
3
(ii) and(iii). Consistent with Theorem
3
(iv) and(v), two leading eigenvectors are outliers and the subfigure of Figure
1
exhibits non-trivial alignment with
{\bm{v}}_{1},{\bm{v}}_{2}
defined in (
3.7
), but remains orthogonal to the XOR-labels
{\bm{y}}
. Consequently, although we can apply additional nonlinear transforms for leading eigenvectors to classify the data, linear classification of XOR via the eigenvectors of the CK fails in this regime. This case is our baseline and then we vary one knob at a time, reporting how the CK spectra change.

6.2
Knob I: Increase SNR

From Theorem
5
, we generate the CK of XOR with
r=\Theta(n^{1/4})
under
\sigma(x)\propto\sqrt{x^{2}+1}-1
, which satisfies
b_{\sigma}=0,c_{\sigma}\neq 0
. Figure
2
depicts the CK spectra and the principal component plots. CK has two
O(1)
quadratic outliers. Since
b_{\sigma}=0
, the diverging linear outliers are not present.
Moreover, the second eigenvector exhibits two-class structure, and XOR becomes linearly classifiable via the CK eigenvectors.

Spectral/algorithmic phase transition as
r
increases.

The results of Section
4.2
imply that there is a phase transition when the SNR surpasses the critical value of
r=\Theta(n^{\frac{1}{4}})
. In this case, the quadratic component of
\sigma
is no longer negligible, and two further eigenvalues emerge from the bulk with eigenvectors that asymptotically align with XOR-labels. To support this, we simulate the CK under increasing SNR in Figure
3
. Plots are drawn with
\sigma(x)\propto\sqrt{x^{2}+1}-1
, which has
b_{\sigma}=0
and
c_{\sigma}\neq 0
. Since
b_{\sigma}=0
, the two linear eigenvalues are not present. Instead only the two
O(1)
quadratic eigenvalues isolate from the bulk. Figure
3
depicts the CK principal component plots of XOR exhibiting a phase transition in SNR, during which XOR becomes linearly separable. This transition roughly coincides with the two quadratic eigenvalues with XOR-label aligned eigenvectors emerging from the bulk of the CK, depicted on the right of Figure
3
.

Figure 3
:
CK Spectra Phase Transition:
(Top)
CK spectra under finite SNR regime
(r=6)
, no eigenvalues separate from the bulk.
(Bottom)
CK spectra under large SNR regime
(r=25)
. Two quadratic eigenvalues separate from bulk, with the second leading eigenvector aligning with XOR-labels. Plots generated with
n=5000,N=d=15000
and
\sigma(x)\propto\sqrt{x^{2}+1}-1
, which satisfies
b_{\sigma}=0,c_{\sigma}\neq 0
.

6.3
Knob II: Activation Function
\sigma

Varying
c_{\sigma}
with
b_{\sigma}=0
: uninformative spikes can emerge.

In order to emphasize the role of non-linearity
\sigma
on the possible emergence of uninformative spikes in the CK, we consider the single-parameter family of activation functions

f_{\alpha}(x)=\frac{\cos(\alpha x)-e^{-\alpha^{2}/2}}{\sqrt{e^{-\alpha^{2}}\bigl((\cosh(\alpha^{2})-1\bigr)}}

(6.1)

from
[
12
]
. These functions satisfy Assumption
1
, with
b_{\sigma}=0
for all choices of
\alpha
while
c_{\sigma}
varies with
\alpha
. Figure
4
plots the spectra of the CK under
f_{\alpha}
and the null model
r=0
for decreasing values of
\alpha
. As
\alpha
decreases two outlier spikes emerge in the CK. These two uninformative spikes correspond to the covariance spike and mean spike from Theorem
3
. The bulk distribution remains MP, which is plotted in red.

Figure 4
:
CK spectra under activation function
f_{\alpha}(x)
defined in (
6.1
) and null model SNR
r=0
. As
\alpha
decreases,
c_{\sigma}
increases and two (uninformative) spikes emerge. The bulk remains close to MP (plotted in red). Plots are generated with
n=6000,N=d=1000
.

Switching off the quadratic channel (
c_{\sigma}=0
): XOR remains non-separable even at large SNR.

On the other hand, if the activation function
\sigma
satisfies
c_{\sigma}=0
the quadratic feature channel in the CK is absent. As discussed in Theorem
5
, this implies the CK cannot be used to linearly classify XOR for any SNR. Figure
5
depicts the principal components of the CK under
\sigma\propto\tanh
for increasing SNR. Even in the large-SNR regime, the two classes in the CK space do not linearly separate. Indeed by Theorem
5
, only two eigenvalues from the linear channel of the CK will isolate from the bulk in the large SNR regime. Moreover, their eigenvectors remain asymptotically orthogonal to XOR-labels
{\bm{y}}
, as depicted in the right side of Figure
5
.

Figure 5
:
Large-SNR regime with an odd activation: separated CK spikes can appear without revealing the XOR labels.

We consider the CK matrix
\bm{K}
with activation
\sigma(x)\propto\tanh(x)
, for which the quadratic Hermite coefficient vanishes (
c_{\sigma}=0
). All settings are the same as in Figure
2
except
\sigma
.
In this case Theorem
5
predicts only
linear
outliers, with no quadratic label-aligned spike.

Left:
kernel-PCA visualization of the samples using the
2nd
and
3rd
PCs of
\bm{K}
(one point per sample, colored by its binary XOR label).
The samples split into four vertical clusters: the two outer clusters belong to the positive class and the two inner clusters belong to the negative class.
Thus the leading nontrivial spectral coordinates remain
not
linearly separable with respect to the XOR labels, despite the presence of isolated spikes.

Right:
empirical eigenvalue histogram of
\bm{K}
(blue bars) together with the theoretical locations of the two isolated linear outliers predicted by the large-SNR asymptotic theory (red
\times
markers).Two spikes appears with very large order and the arrows connect these predicted outlier locations to the corresponding empirical outlier eigenvectors plotted against sample index.
Their approximately piecewise-constant four-block pattern reflects the four Gaussian-mixture components (equivalently, the two underlying coordinate-sign directions) rather than the binary XOR partition.
Consequently, the outlier eigenspace is spectrally visible but asymptotically orthogonal to the XOR label vector
{\bm{y}}
.

6.4
Knob III: Weight Structure

Spiked weight case: controlled feature injection into
\bm{W}_{1}
.

We generate a rank-one spike to the random initialized weights
\bm{W}_{1}=\bm{W}+\theta{\bm{a}}{\bm{b}}^{\top}
, and plot the CK and its principal components in Figure
6
, under
\sigma(x)\propto\mathrm{ReLU}
and XOR with SNR
r=6.0
. As Theorem
6
predicts, the leading eigenvalue is
O(\sqrt{N})
. The second and third spiked eigenvalues remain
O(1)
, with the second leading eigenvector exhibiting two-class structure, which allows for linear classification of XOR in the CK space.

Figure 6
:
Spiked weight regime: a dominant weight-induced spike coexists aligned with XOR labels.

We consider the CK matrix
\bm{K}
built from the spiked weight
\bm{W}_{1}=\bm{W}+\theta{\bm{a}}{\bm{b}}^{\top}
.
where the rank-one perturbation creates an additional large spectral direction for XOR linear classification.

Left:
kernel-PCA visualization of the samples using the
2nd
and
3rd
PCs of
K
(each point is one sample, colored by its true binary label).
These two coordinates linearly separate the positive and negative classes, indicating that the informative label-aligned structure is carried by the second and third spectral directions.

Right:
empirical eigenvalue histogram of
\bm{K}
(blue bars) together with the isolated eigenvalue locations predicted by Theorem
6
(red
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\times}
markers).
The arrows link the two informative outliers to the empirical eigenvectors shown in the subfigure: empirical eigenvectors
2
(green) and
3
(blue) plotted against sample index.
The far-right red marker corresponds to the largest spike induced by the rank-one perturbation in the weights; this dominant direction is separated from the two lower informative spikes and is therefore not used in the left-panel embedding.
Simulation parameters:
N=n=8000
,
d=4000
, SNR
r=6.0
, activation
\sigma(x)\propto\mathrm{ReLU}
, and weight spike

\theta=18.0,\ b=\mathbf{1}_{d},\ {\bm{a}}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\tfrac{1}{N}\bm{I}_{N}).

Pretrained weights: feature learning induces label-aligned CK eigenvectors.

From the spiked weight studied in Section
4.3
, we consider the CK under pretrained weights extracted from a 20-layer NN trained on the CIFAR-2 (40 epochs with final test accuracy
88\%
). Figure
7
depicts 1st layer trained weight and its CK spectra tested on XOR. The emergence of spikes in the spectra of weights during the training process suggests the model is learning task-dependent features, a phenomena known as feature learning
[
31
]
in NNs. In contrast to Theorem
3
, the pretrained CK exhibits four spikes, with the fourth leading eigenvector aligning with XOR-labels.
After sufficient training, the fourth leading eigenvector transitions from pure noise to near-perfect alignment with XOR-labels, enabling linear classification. Our experiment suggests that under certain training dynamics and parameter tuning, there exists a critical phase transition in learning parallel to a spectral transition. See Appendix
A
for further details.

Figure 7
:
CIFAR-2 pretrained model:
(Left)
ESD of layer FC1 weights after 40 epochs. Overlaid in red is the MP distribution.
(Right)
ESD of CK with pretrained weights. Plots in this section are generated with
n=10000,d=3072,N=512
and XOR with SNR
r=5.25
under
\sigma(x)\propto\mathrm{ReLU}
.

6.5
Knob IV: Sample Size
n

Quadratic sample-size regime (
n=\Theta(d^{2})
).

Following Section
4.4
, we plot the CK under the quadratic scaling regime
n=\Theta(d^{2})
. Figure
8
depicts the CK spectra and principal component plots of the CK under
\sigma(x)\propto\mathrm{ReLU}
and XOR with SNR
r=6.0
. Emphasis on the fourth leading eigenvalue, whose eigenvector aligns with XOR-labels
{\bm{y}}
. Plots are generated in the linear width
N\asymp n
regime, and without the assumption that
b_{\sigma}=0
. A future direction of this work is to extend Theorem
7
to this more general setting.

Figure 8
:
Quadratic sample-size regime: informative CK spikes become visible beyond the leading spectral directions.

We consider the CK matrix
\bm{K}
in the quadratic scaling regime
n=\Theta(d^{2})
, where quadratic-order structure of the XOR model generates isolated, label-aligned spikes.

Left:
kernel-PCA visualization of the samples using the
3rd
and
4th
PCs of
\bm{K}
(each point is one sample, colored by its true binary label).
These selected spectral coordinates separate the positive and negative classes by a linear boundary.

Right:
empirical spectrum of
\bm{K}
shown as a histogram (blue bars), together with the asymptotic spike predictions from Theorem
7
(red
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\times}
markers).
The histogram exhibits several distinct spectral scales, including a bulk near the origin and multiple isolated spikes. There are two extreme large isolated spikes in order one and two moderate outliers whose eigenvectors are displayed in the subfigure: empirical eigenvectors
3
(green) and

(blue) plotted against sample index.

,

, SNR

, and

.

7
Conclusion

We have introduced a quadratic equivalent framework and illustrated its use by characterizing when nonlinear structure becomes spectrally visible in the CK matrix.
For the nonlinear XOR problem, we show that in the proportional limit with finite SNR, the CK can exhibit outliers, but their eigenvectors are asymptotically unaligned with labels.
In contrast, by increasing SNR, sample complexity, or adding pretrained structure in weight, the quadratic nonlinearity contributes emergence of quadratic informative outliers that enable linear spectral classification.
Our results highlight the complex and subtle relationship between common ML training “knobs” and the emergence of spikes in the spectrum of ML models.
Extending these quadratic equivalents to more general scalings, deeper networks, and other forms of structured data is an interesting direction.

Contents

Appendix A
CIFAR Pretrained Weights Experiment Details

A recent line of works
[
59
,
57
]
have tracked the evolution of the Empirical Spectral Distribution (ESD) of weight matrices throughout the training process of Deep Neural Networks. We focus on two main phases of ESD during training: the so-called ”Bulk+Spikes” model, and Bulk-Decay model. The emergence of spikes in the weight spectra is strongly related to the feature-learning ability of neural networks
[
31
]
, and suggests the spikes carry task-relevant information.

In order to study the spectra of the CK under pretrained weights, we trained a
L=20
-layer fully connected Neural Network on the CIFAR image dataset, with input dimension
d_{0}=3072
and hidden dimensions
N_{\ell}=512,
for
\ell=1,\cdots,L
. To extract binary-classification relevant features from the trained weights, we restrict the CIFAR-10 dataset to two classes. The model was trained using stochastic gradient descent (SGD) with momentum, with a learning rate
\text{lr}=0.05
and batch size
b=128
. Our model achieved approximately
88\%
accuracy on an independent test set after 40 epochs of training. Figure
9
depicts the spectra of FC1 layer weights, tracked over each pass of the training dataset. Early phases of training exhibit the ”Bulk+Spikes” model: the bulk distribution remains close to random MP initialization, with several outlier spikes beginning to emerge past the right edge of the MP distribution. In later training phases, the bulk begins to bleed out from the MP distribution, with significant outlier spikes emerging.

Our results empirically suggest that the LE approximation of the CK breaks down even during the early stages of training. Indeed, XOR becomes almost linearly separable in the CK component space after just one epoch of training. After further training, a spiked eigenvalue emerges with XOR-label aligned eigenvector. Figure
10
depicts the fourth leading eigenvector of the CK under weights frozen after each epoch of training, with a phase transition occurring between Epoch 20 and 30. The results of these experiments suggests that under certain training regimes and parameter tuning, there exists a critical phase transition in the learning process for which the emergence of spikes in weights becomes sufficient for quadratic channels in the CK to become non-negligible. One possible explanation is that training the neural network induces spiked eigenvalues with eigenvectors that align with XOR labels, even though the model is not trained on the XOR problem directly. Theorem
6
models this by artificially introducing a large spike in the weights, which induces a spiked CK eigenvalue with XOR-label aligned eigenvector.

Figure 9
:
Spectra over training: Zoomed in ESD of the CIFAR-2 trained weights
\widetilde{}\bm{W}_{1}
tracked through training from Epoch
0
to Epoch
40
. MP distribution overlaid in red. By epoch 40, significant spikes begin to bleed out from the bulk distribution. Plots in this section are generated with
n=10000,d=3072,N=512
.

Figure 10
:
Eigenvector alignment over training: Fourth leading eigenvector of CK with pre-trained weights
\widetilde{}\bm{W}_{1}
and XOR with SNR
r=6
plotted over training. Weights are frozen after each step of gradient descent, to emphasize phase transition that occurs around step 3, leading to non-trivial alignment with XOR labels
{\bm{y}}
.

Appendix B
Preliminary Lemmas and Propositions

Throughout this paper, we utilize the stochastic domination notation introduced by
[
27
]
.

Definition 9

(Stochastic domination)
.

Consider any two families of random variables

X=\{X_{n}(u):n\in\mathbb{N},u\in U_{n}\},\,\,Y=\{Y_{n}(u):n\in\mathbb{N},u\in U_{n}\},

with parameter
u\in U_{n}
where
U_{n}
is a possibly
n
-dependent deterministic set. Let
\mathcal{E}=\{E_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence of measure subsets, where each
E_{n}
is related to
X_{n}(u)
and
Y_{n}(u)
.

1.

We denote
|X|\prec Y
or
X=O_{\prec}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Y}}\right)
as the stochastic domination of
X
by
Y
uniformly in
u\in U_{n}
: if for all small
\epsilon>0
and large
D>0
, there exists some
N_{0}(\epsilon,D)
, such that for all
n\geq N_{0}(\epsilon,D)
,

\sup_{u\in U_{n}}\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(|X_{n}(u)|\geq n^{\epsilon}Y_{n}(u)}}\right)\leq n^{-D}.

2.

For simplicity, for a family of random matrices
\bm{A}
and a family of non-negative random variables
\zeta
,
\bm{A}=O_{\prec}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\zeta}}\right)
represents
|\langle{\bm{v}},\bm{A}{\bm{u}}\rangle|\prec\zeta\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{{\bm{v}}}}}\right\|\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{{\bm{u}}}}}\right\|
uniformly for all deterministic vectors
{\bm{v}}
and
{\bm{u}}
.

If
\{\bm{A}_{n}\}
are random matrices, we interpret

\|\bm{A}_{n}\|_{\mathrm{op}}=o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\|\bm{A}_{n}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\xrightarrow{\mathbb{P}}0.

For simplicity, in the proofs, we use
\|\bm{A}_{n}\|
to represent operator norm
\|\bm{A}_{n}\|_{\mathrm{op}}
.

B.1
Properties of XOR Data

We introduce the following notion of
(\tau_{n},B)-
orthonormality
for our feature matrix
[
29
]
.

Definition 10
.

A matrix
\bm{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times n}
is
(\tau_{n},B)-
orthonormal
if the columns
\{{\bm{x}}_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha=1}^{n}
of
\bm{X}
satisfy

\bigl\lvert\|{\bm{x}}_{\alpha}\|-1\bigr\rvert\leq\tau_{n},\quad\bigl\lvert\|{\bm{x}}_{\beta}\|-1\bigr\rvert\leq\tau_{n},\quad\bigl\lvert{\bm{x}}_{\alpha}^{\top}{\bm{x}}_{\beta}\bigr\rvert\leq\tau_{n}

(B.1)

for all pairs
\alpha\neq\beta\in[n]
, and also

\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\bigl(\|{\bm{x}}_{\alpha}\|-1\bigr)^{2}\leq B^{2},\qquad\|\bm{X}\|\leq B.

Lemma 11
.

Let
\bm{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times n}
be the XOR data matrix:

\bm{X}=\bm{Z}+\bm{M},\qquad(\bm{Z})_{ij}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}\!\Big(0,\frac{1}{d}\Big),\qquad\bm{M}=\theta\,\bm{u}_{1}\bm{v}_{1}^{\top}+\theta\,\bm{u}_{2}\bm{v}_{2}^{\top},\qquad\theta_{\mathrm{SNR}}=r\sqrt{\frac{n}{2d}},

where
\bm{u}_{1},\bm{u}_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\bm{v}_{1},\bm{v}_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
are orthonormal vectors defined by (
3.7
).
Assume either

(A1)

n\asymp d
and
r=O(n^{1/4})
,  or

(A2)

n=O(d^{2})
and
r=O(1)
.

Then there exist constants
c,C>0

such that with probability at least
1-n^{-c}
, the matrix
\bm{X}
satisfies (
B.1
) with

\tau_{n}\;=\;C\Big(\sqrt{\frac{\log n}{d}}+\frac{r^{2}}{d}\Big).

(B.2)

Moreover, on the same event,

\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\big(\|\bm{x}_{\alpha}\|-1\big)^{2}\;\leq\;\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\big(\|\bm{x}_{\alpha}\|^{2}-1\big)^{2}\;\leq\;C\Big(\frac{n}{d}+\frac{nr^{2}}{d^{2}}+\frac{nr^{4}}{d^{2}}\Big),

(B.3)

and also

\|\bm{X}\|\leq C\Big(1+(1+r)\sqrt{\frac{n}{d}}\Big).

(B.4)

In particular, in regime (A1) one may take
\tau_{n}=C\sqrt{\frac{\log n}{n}}
and
B=C(1+r)
, and in regime (A2)
one may take
\tau_{n}=C\sqrt{\frac{\log n}{d}}
and
B=C\sqrt{\frac{n}{d}}
such that
\bm{X}
is
(\tau_{n},B)-
orthonormal
with probability at least
1-n^{-c}
.

Proof.

Throughout the proof,
c,C>0
denote constants that may change from line to line. This proof is basically following the proof of Proposition 3.3 of
[
29
]
.

Write the columns as
\bm{x}_{\alpha}=\bm{z}_{\alpha}+\bm{m}_{\alpha}
, where
\bm{z}_{\alpha}
is the
\alpha
-th column of
\bm{Z}

and
\bm{m}_{\alpha}
is the
\alpha
-th column of
\bm{M}
.
Since
\bm{M}=\theta_{\mathrm{SNR}}\,\bm{u}_{1}\bm{v}_{1}^{\top}+\theta_{\mathrm{SNR}}\,\bm{u}_{2}\bm{v}_{2}^{\top}
and

\mathrm{supp}(\bm{v}_{1})\cap\mathrm{supp}(\bm{v}_{2})=\emptyset
, for each
\alpha
exactly one of

\bm{v}_{1}[\alpha],\bm{v}_{2}[\alpha]
is nonzero, and we have

\bm{m}_{\alpha}=\theta_{\mathrm{SNR}}\,\bm{u}_{1}\bm{v}_{1}[\alpha]\ \ \text{or}\ \ \theta_{\mathrm{SNR}}\,\bm{u}_{2}\bm{v}_{2}[\alpha].

Using
|\bm{v}_{1}[\alpha]|=|\bm{v}_{2}[\alpha]|=\sqrt{2/n}
on the support, we obtain for all
\alpha

\|\bm{m}_{\alpha}\|=\theta_{\mathrm{SNR}}\sqrt{\frac{2}{n}}=r\sqrt{\frac{n}{2d}}\sqrt{\frac{2}{n}}=\frac{r}{\sqrt{d}},\qquad\|\bm{m}_{\alpha}\|^{2}=\frac{r^{2}}{d},\qquad\|\bm{m}_{\alpha}\|^{4}=\frac{r^{4}}{d^{2}}.

(B.5)

Now we verify the four requirements in the
(\tau_{n},B)
-orthonormal definition:
(i)
\max_{\alpha}|\|\bm{x}_{\alpha}\|-1|\leq\tau_{n}
,
(ii)
\max_{\alpha\neq\beta}|\bm{x}_{\alpha}^{\top}\bm{x}_{\beta}|\leq\tau_{n}
,
(iii)
\sum_{\alpha}(\|\bm{x}_{\alpha}\|^{2}-1)^{2}\leq B^{2}
,
(iv)
\|\bm{X}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq B
.

Step 1: Uniform control of column norms.

For each
\alpha\in[n]
,

\|\bm{x}_{\alpha}\|^{2}=\|\bm{z}_{\alpha}\|^{2}+2\,\bm{z}_{\alpha}^{\top}\bm{m}_{\alpha}+\|\bm{m}_{\alpha}\|^{2}.

(B.6)

(1a) Noise norm term.

Since
\bm{z}_{\alpha}\sim\mathcal{N}(\bm{0},\bm{I}_{d}/d)
, we have
d\|\bm{z}_{\alpha}\|^{2}\sim\chi_{d}^{2}
.
By Lemma 1 of
[
48
]
: for all
t>0
,

\mathbb{P}\Big(\big|\|\bm{z}_{\alpha}\|^{2}-1\big|\geq 2\sqrt{\tfrac{t}{d}}+2\tfrac{t}{d}\Big)\leq 2e^{-t}.

(B.7)

Taking
t=C_{0}\log n
and union bounding over
\alpha\in[n]
yields: with probability at least

1-2n\cdot n^{-C_{0}}
, i.e.
1-n^{-c}
for
C_{0}
large enough,

\max_{\alpha\in[n]}\big|\|\bm{z}_{\alpha}\|^{2}-1\big|\;\leq\;C\sqrt{\frac{\log n}{d}}.

(B.8)

In particular, on the same event,
\max_{\alpha}\|\bm{z}_{\alpha}\|^{2}\leq 2
for all large
n
.

(1b) Cross term.

Conditioning on
\bm{m}_{\alpha}
, the scalar
\bm{z}_{\alpha}^{\top}\bm{m}_{\alpha}
is Gaussian with

\bm{z}_{\alpha}^{\top}\bm{m}_{\alpha}\,\big|\,\bm{m}_{\alpha}\sim\mathcal{N}\!\Big(0,\frac{\|\bm{m}_{\alpha}\|^{2}}{d}\Big)=\mathcal{N}\!\Big(0,\frac{r^{2}}{d^{2}}\Big),

using (
B.5
).
Hence for
t=C_{0}\log n
,

\mathbb{P}\Big(|\bm{z}_{\alpha}^{\top}\bm{m}_{\alpha}|\geq\frac{r}{d}\sqrt{2t}\Big)\leq 2e^{-t}.

A union bound over
\alpha\in[n]
yields: with probability at least
1-n^{-c}
,

\max_{\alpha\in[n]}|\bm{z}_{\alpha}^{\top}\bm{m}_{\alpha}|\;\leq\;C\,\frac{r\sqrt{\log n}}{d}.

(B.9)

(1c) Combine.

Plugging (
B.8
), (
B.9
), and
\|\bm{m}_{\alpha}\|^{2}=r^{2}/d
into
(
B.6
) gives, with probability at least
1-n^{-c}
,

\max_{\alpha\in[n]}\big|\|\bm{x}_{\alpha}\|^{2}-1\big|\;\leq\;C\Big(\sqrt{\frac{\log n}{d}}+\frac{r\sqrt{\log n}}{d}+\frac{r^{2}}{d}\Big).

(B.10)

We now simplify the RHS under regimes (A1) and (A2). In both regimes, for large
n
,

\frac{r\sqrt{\log n}}{d}\;\leq\;\sqrt{\frac{\log n}{d}}+\frac{r^{2}}{d}.

Indeed:

•

If
r^{2}\geq\sqrt{\log n}
, then
\frac{r\sqrt{\log n}}{d}\leq\frac{r^{2}}{d}
.

•

If
r^{2}\leq\sqrt{\log n}
, then
r\leq(\log n)^{1/4}
so

\frac{r\sqrt{\log n}}{d}\leq\frac{(\log n)^{3/4}}{d}\leq\sqrt{\frac{\log n}{d}}
for all large
d
.

Therefore (
B.10
) implies

\max_{\alpha\in[n]}\big|\|\bm{x}_{\alpha}\|^{2}-1\big|\;\leq\;C\Big(\sqrt{\frac{\log n}{d}}+\frac{r^{2}}{d}\Big).

(B.11)

Finally, convert squared-norm deviation to norm deviation: since
\|\bm{x}_{\alpha}\|+1\geq 1
,

\big|\|\bm{x}_{\alpha}\|-1\big|=\frac{\big|\|\bm{x}_{\alpha}\|^{2}-1\big|}{\|\bm{x}_{\alpha}\|+1}\leq\big|\|\bm{x}_{\alpha}\|^{2}-1\big|,

so (
B.11
) yields

\max_{\alpha\in[n]}\big|\|\bm{x}_{\alpha}\|-1\big|\;\leq\;C\Big(\sqrt{\frac{\log n}{d}}+\frac{r^{2}}{d}\Big).

(B.12)

This verifies the column-norm part of
(\tau_{n},B)
-orthonormality with
\tau_{n}
as in (
B.2
).

Step 2: Uniform control of pairwise inner products.

Fix
\alpha\neq\beta\in[n]
. Expand

\bm{x}_{\alpha}^{\top}\bm{x}_{\beta}=\bm{z}_{\alpha}^{\top}\bm{z}_{\beta}+\bm{z}_{\alpha}^{\top}\bm{m}_{\beta}+\bm{m}_{\alpha}^{\top}\bm{z}_{\beta}+\bm{m}_{\alpha}^{\top}\bm{m}_{\beta}.

(B.13)

(2a) Noise–noise term.

The scalar
\bm{z}_{\alpha}^{\top}\bm{z}_{\beta}
is not exactly Gaussian, but it is conditionally Gaussian:
conditioning on
\bm{z}_{\alpha}
,

\bm{z}_{\alpha}^{\top}\bm{z}_{\beta}\,\big|\,\bm{z}_{\alpha}\sim\mathcal{N}\!\Big(0,\frac{\|\bm{z}_{\alpha}\|^{2}}{d}\Big).

On the event
\max_{\alpha}\|\bm{z}_{\alpha}\|^{2}\leq 2
from Step 1, we have conditional variance
\leq 2/d
and hence

\mathbb{P}\Big(|\bm{z}_{\alpha}^{\top}\bm{z}_{\beta}|\geq t\ \Big|\ \bm{z}_{\alpha}\Big)\leq 2\exp\Big(-\frac{dt^{2}}{4\|\bm{z}_{\alpha}\|^{2}}\Big)\leq 2\exp\Big(-c\,dt^{2}\Big).

Taking
t=C\sqrt{\frac{\log n}{d}}
and union bounding over all
\alpha\neq\beta\in[n]
(at most
n^{2}
pairs) yields
with probability at least
1-n^{-c}
,

\max_{\alpha\neq\beta}|\bm{z}_{\alpha}^{\top}\bm{z}_{\beta}|\;\leq\;C\sqrt{\frac{\log n}{d}}.

(B.14)

(2b) Noise–signal terms.

For
\alpha\neq\beta
, conditional on
\bm{m}_{\beta}
,

\bm{z}_{\alpha}^{\top}\bm{m}_{\beta}\sim\mathcal{N}\!\Big(0,\frac{\|\bm{m}_{\beta}\|^{2}}{d}\Big)=\mathcal{N}\!\Big(0,\frac{r^{2}}{d^{2}}\Big),

and similarly
\bm{m}_{\alpha}^{\top}\bm{z}_{\beta}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,r^{2}/d^{2})
.
A union bound over all
\alpha\neq\beta
gives, with probability at least
1-n^{-c}
,

\max_{\alpha\neq\beta}|\bm{z}_{\alpha}^{\top}\bm{m}_{\beta}|\;\leq\;C\,\frac{r\sqrt{\log n}}{d},\qquad\max_{\alpha\neq\beta}|\bm{m}_{\alpha}^{\top}\bm{z}_{\beta}|\;\leq\;C\,\frac{r\sqrt{\log n}}{d}.

(B.15)

(2c) Signal–signal term.

Each
\bm{m}_{\alpha}
equals
\pm\frac{r}{\sqrt{d}}\bm{u}_{1}
or
\pm\frac{r}{\sqrt{d}}\bm{u}_{2}
. By Cauchy–Schwarz inequality,

|\bm{m}_{\alpha}^{\top}\bm{m}_{\beta}|\leq\|\bm{m}_{\alpha}\|\|\bm{m}_{\beta}\|=\frac{r^{2}}{d}.

Hence

\max_{\alpha\neq\beta}|\bm{m}_{\alpha}^{\top}\bm{m}_{\beta}|\;\leq\;\frac{r^{2}}{d}.

(B.16)

(2d) Combine.

Combining (
B.13
)–(
B.16
) and using the same simplification as in Step 1,
we obtain with probability at least
1-n^{-c}
,

\max_{\alpha\neq\beta}|\bm{x}_{\alpha}^{\top}\bm{x}_{\beta}|\;\leq\;C\Big(\sqrt{\frac{\log n}{d}}+\frac{r^{2}}{d}\Big).

(B.17)

This verifies the pairwise-inner-product part of
(\tau_{n},B)
-orthonormality with
\tau_{n}
as in (
B.2
).

Step 3: Sum of squared squared-norm deviations.

From (
B.6
),

\|\bm{x}_{\alpha}\|^{2}-1=(\|\bm{z}_{\alpha}\|^{2}-1)+2\,\bm{z}_{\alpha}^{\top}\bm{m}_{\alpha}+\|\bm{m}_{\alpha}\|^{2}.

Using
(a+b+c)^{2}\leq 3(a^{2}+b^{2}+c^{2})
, we get

(\|\bm{x}_{\alpha}\|^{2}-1)^{2}\leq 3(\|\bm{z}_{\alpha}\|^{2}-1)^{2}+12(\bm{z}_{\alpha}^{\top}\bm{m}_{\alpha})^{2}+3\|\bm{m}_{\alpha}\|^{4}.

Summing over
\alpha
and using (
B.5
) gives

\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}(\|\bm{x}_{\alpha}\|^{2}-1)^{2}\leq 3\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}(\|\bm{z}_{\alpha}\|^{2}-1)^{2}+12\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}(\bm{z}_{\alpha}^{\top}\bm{m}_{\alpha})^{2}+3n\frac{r^{4}}{d^{2}}.

(B.18)

(3a) Control of
\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}(\|{\bm{z}}_{\alpha}\|^{2}-1)^{2}
.

Set
g_{\alpha}:=\sqrt{d}\,{\bm{z}}_{\alpha}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})
so that

\|{\bm{z}}_{\alpha}\|^{2}=\|g_{\alpha}\|^{2}/d
and
\|g_{\alpha}\|^{2}\sim\chi_{d}^{2}
.
Define

U_{\alpha}:=\|g_{\alpha}\|^{2}-d,\qquad X_{\alpha}:=(\|{\bm{z}}_{\alpha}\|^{2}-1)^{2}=\frac{U_{\alpha}^{2}}{d^{2}}.

Then
\mathbb{E}[X_{\alpha}]=\mathrm{Var}(\|{\bm{z}}_{\alpha}\|^{2})=2/d
, hence
\mathbb{E}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}X_{\alpha}=2n/d
.

Moment bound.

Write
U_{\alpha}=\sum_{i=1}^{d}(\xi_{i\alpha}^{2}-1)
with
\xi_{i\alpha}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,1)
.
For any integer
p\geq 2
, Rosenthal’s inequality (Theorem 3 of
[
71
]
) yields

\mathbb{E}|U_{\alpha}|^{2p}\ \leq\ C_{p}\Big(d\,\mathbb{E}|\xi_{11}^{2}-1|^{2p}+(d\,\mathrm{Var}(\xi_{11}^{2}-1))^{p}\Big)\ \leq\ C_{p}^{\prime}d^{p},

(B.19)

where
C_{p},C_{p}^{\prime}
depend only on
p
(not on
d,n
